## AI Research

### [The Darwinian Honeymoon - Why I am not as impressed by human progress as I used to be](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FxHzT6jeTRhbkzSX3/the-darwinian-honeymoon-why-i-am-not-as-impressed-by-human-1)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><span>Crossposted from </span></i><a href="https://open.substack.com/pub/eliasschmied/p/why-i-am-not-as-impressed-by-human?r=7ta4d&amp;utm_campaign=post&amp;utm_medium=web&amp;showWelcomeOnShare=true" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Substack </span></i></a><i><span>and the </span></i><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/uWZQgYF69AAAZt7ky/the-darwinian-honeymoon-why-i-am-not-as-impressed-by-human" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>EA Forum</span></i></a><i><span>.</span></i></p><p><br /></p><p><span>A common argument for optimism about the future is that living conditions have improved a lot in the past few hundred years, billions of people have been lifted out of poverty, and so on. It’s a very strong, grounding piece of evidence - probably the best we have in figuring out what our foundational beliefs about the world should be.</span></p><p><span>However, I now think it’s a lot less powerful than I once did.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Let’s take a Darwinian perspective - entities that are better at reproducing, spreading and power-seeking will become more common and eventually dominate the world.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefha523ztj2k"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnha523ztj2k">[1]</a></sup></span><span> This is an almost tautological story that plausibly applies to everything ever, agnostic to the specifics. It first happened with biological life in the last few billion years and humans specifically in the last hundred thousand years. Eventually, it led to accelerating economic growth in the last few thousand years, and in the future it will presumably lead to the colonization of the universe.</span></p><p><span>My core point is this: It makes complete sense that this nihilistic optimization process at first actually benefits some class of agent - because initially, the easiest way to keep growing is to use some class of agent in the world and incentivize it by satisfying its preferences. But then, as the optimization becomes more and more advanced, it stops being beneficial - because there are almost certainly some more evolutionarily fit configurations out there than the class of agent that the process just happened to start out with. It’s a “Darwinian honeymoon”.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefvg1v3em4m4a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnvg1v3em4m4a">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>That’s very abstract - I’ll give an example. The population of the red junglefowl, the wild ancestor of the chicken, was in maybe the tens of millions, concentrated in the Southeast Asian jungle.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdfajosa30sk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndfajosa30sk">[3]</a></sup></span><span> Then, around seven thousand years ago, humans started to domesticate them into </span><i><span>Gallus Gallus domesticus</span></i><span>, the modern chicken, and chicken farming spread through Persia and the Middle East into Europe. By 1700, there were maybe a billion chickens in existence.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftolrbidy37p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntolrbidy37p">[4]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>At that point - before the Industrial Revolution began - poultry farming was still a small-scale, household affair. Chickens were kept in small wooden coops or henhouses at night to protect them from predators, and let out to free-range during the day. Around half of chickens were surplus roosters, young males not needed for breeding and killed at 4–6 months of age. The other half were mostly laying hens, killed at 2–4 years of age. The most common methods of killing were neck wringing and beheading - both of which result in instant death if done correctly.</span></p><p><span>This was an enormous increase in the total welfare of poultry: a 100x increase in population, being given extra food by humans, </span><i><span>and </span></i><span>far more painless deaths than in the wild (where they died from predation, disease, and only rarely old age).</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefdgrvo4pbru7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fndgrvo4pbru7">[5]</a></sup></span><span> From the chickens’ perspective, it would have been an unimaginable, mindboggling blessing. Heaven on Earth. Really take in how extraordinary this is - it’s probably larger than the increase in human welfare from 1800 to today that we are all so excited about! (which involved only an 8x increase in population)</span></p><p><span>Early domestication was intended for cockfighting to a surprising extent (with use for meat and eggs only starting later), an activity presumably very distressing for the roosters. So there was even moral progress over time!</span></p><p><span>Chicken intellectuals would probably have been saying stuff like this:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="grafik.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778424825/lexical_client_uploads/n3owsw4jbmm5rubrw5ek.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>“Maybe us and our human friends will colonize the universe together, and transform the trillions of planets of the Milky Way into endless spacious coops to cluck around in. What a bright future we have!”</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>Then… things changed.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Today, male chicks born at egg-laying facilities are useless to the industry and are killed on their first day of life - typically by being fed alive into high-speed grinders (maceration) or suffocated with CO₂, with billions killed this way every year globally. Female chicks have a portion of their highly sensitive, nerve-dense beaks cut or burned off without anesthetic within days of hatching, to prevent them from stress-pecking each other. Broiler chickens have been selectively bred to grow so fast - reaching slaughter weight in around 6 weeks - that their hearts, lungs, and legs frequently cannot support their own bodies, leaving many unable to walk, suffering heart attacks, or dying from organ failure before they even reach slaughter. Those in conventional housing live on floors covered in accumulated feces, causing chronic ammonia burns to their feet, legs, and eyes. Laying hens spend their entire lives in battery cages with roughly 61 square inches of space each - less than a single sheet of A4 paper - unable to spread their wings, turn around, or take a single step in any direction. When egg production slows, they are sometimes intentionally starved for up to two weeks in a practice called “forced molting”, to shock their bodies into another laying cycle. At slaughter, chickens are shackled upside down while fully conscious, dragged through an electrified water bath, and passed through an automated throat-cutting blade - a process with a well-documented failure rate. The ones that are improperly stunned or miss the blade entirely proceed directly into scalding tanks of near-boiling water, where they are drowned or boiled alive while conscious.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefotdbzyfq2x"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnotdbzyfq2x">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Chickens had normal, happy lives for most of history because humans needed to “cooperate” with them, in a way - there was no alternative to free-range, and confining them in tight spaces would have made disease too much of a problem. Chickens had some negotiating power, so to speak, just by virtue of existing in their natural form, with their natural preferences.</span></p><p><span>But then, humans invented antibiotics and vaccines to make extreme density viable. They synthesized vitamin D and added it to feed to make sunlight unnecessary. They discovered mechanized processing made farms with millions of birds possible. They figured out breeding science to grow chickens to unhealthy sizes. They found the right culture, and enough psychological distance in the industrial process, to disregard the chickens’ suffering.</span></p><p><span>The world optimized itself.</span></p><hr /><p><span>People sometimes use chimpanzees as an analogy for AI - we will be to AIs like chimpanzees are to us, and look at the difference in power there. But this can feel dissonant with the fact that humans are </span><i><span>obviously </span></i><span>doing amazing right now, in a way that apes never were.</span></p><p><span>The chicken analogy may help bridge the gap. Chickens were doing </span><i><span>so well </span></i><span>- until they weren’t.</span></p><p><span>This makes it feel a lot less surprising that humans are doing much better than 200 years ago, and returns us some extent to a state of ignorance about the future.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreforf442sjhxp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnorf442sjhxp">[7]</a></sup></span><span> </span><i><span>Of course </span></i><span>we’d be doing well for a while - </span><i><span>that’s how this stuff works</span></i><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefff9wfruon8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnff9wfruon8">[8]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3v4vajd6ult"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3v4vajd6ult">[9]</a></sup></span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftnmrrdjmjj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntnmrrdjmjj">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>You might just be in the honeymoon phase.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnha523ztj2k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefha523ztj2k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>People that have talked about this being potentially The Most Important Thing for thinking about the long-term future and influenced me: </span><a href="https://nickbostrom.com/fut/evolution"><span>Nick Bostrom</span></a><span>, Robin Hanson, Allan Dafoe </span><a href="https://www.dropbox.com/scl/fi/winrkiest7swijksv17l6/Dafoe_2015_STHV_On_Technological_Determinism.pdf?rlkey=s82fe8b6t954a3x4dc03a2qx8&amp;e=1&amp;dl=0"><span>here</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://docs.google.com/document/d/1B77VWaXG-u34nSRFKV14pJNHJHHb6sa5zJ08J70CVVA/edit?tab=t.0"><span>here</span></a><span> (I’d recommend the latter), </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.16200"><span>Dan Hendrycks</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://reflectivedisequilibrium.blogspot.com/2012/09/spreading-happiness-to-stars-seems.html"><span>Carl Shulman</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://qri.org/blog/universal-plot#the-consciousness-vs.-pure-replicators-worldview."><span>Andrés Gómez-Emilsson</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnvg1v3em4m4a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefvg1v3em4m4a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It’s also just Goodhart’s Law, I think.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndfajosa30sk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdfajosa30sk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The source for all facts in this post is Claude Sonnet 4.6, and it’s factchecked by another instance of Claude Sonnet 4.6</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntolrbidy37p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftolrbidy37p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>A lot of uncertainty on these numbers, obviously.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fndgrvo4pbru7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefdgrvo4pbru7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Quick note: I haven’t thought/read about animal welfare, and especially wild animal welfare, much. I take suffering-focused views like </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=RyA_eF7W02s&amp;rco=1"><span>Brian Tomasik’s</span></a><span> seriously. (and chickens are r-selected and prey animals, which may be bad?). So I am very uncertain in general.</span></p><p><span>But here, I’m mostly saying that from a “common sense” point of view, these lives seem alright - just like human lives with a distressing death at the end seem alright. So if you believe the human progress prior I’m arguing against, you should also believe this.</span></p><p><span>The other major counterargument I can think of is that the individual chickens die earlier, especially with the many unneeded roosters. The red junglefowl, in contrast, lives 5 to 8 years in the wild. But from a total utilitarian perspective, this is not that relevant.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnotdbzyfq2x"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefotdbzyfq2x">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Claude wrote this paragraph with some editing and guidance from me.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnorf442sjhxp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreforf442sjhxp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In a more mundane context, all this also made me more sympathetic to left-wing skepticism of unrestrained capitalism. We just can’t control it or know where it’s going, long-term.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnff9wfruon8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefff9wfruon8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Also, experts tend to think that the Agricultural Revolution (and possibly even the Industrial Revolution for a bit) reduced aggregate human welfare at first. So the outside view of “humans will keep doing better” has failed before - it’s strictly weaker than the outside view of “things will become more optimized”.</span></p><p><span>My guess is that the total welfare of the world today is probably negative anyway due to factory farming, so that’s another way the optimistic outside view, globally speaking, doesn’t get off the ground.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3v4vajd6ult"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3v4vajd6ult">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The big counterargument is that humans are much more powerful and competent than chickens were - we controlled their lives, after all, and it made it easy for us to “disempower” them. Of course, I agree that the situation is different - the “humans will keep becoming more powerful” outside view fares a lot better than the “humans will keep doing better” outside view - even the Agricultural and Industrial Revolutions involved increases in power for the people at the top. That’s the big question - if humans have enough power and competence to stave things off.</span></p><p><span>I guess I’m trying to shift the frame - getting outcompeted is the </span><i><span>default outcome, </span></i><span>unless something weird and unprecedented happens (permanent alignment, value lock-in, stable global singleton, something).</span></p><p><span>(And at the end of the day, power is not the end goal - welfare is. So it should also worry you that </span><i><span>that</span></i><span> outside view doesn’t seem very strong at all. That’s more in the direction of something like authoritarianism risks.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntnmrrdjmjj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftnmrrdjmjj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Very quick, very speculative, lightly held thoughts on how the honeymoon may already be ending: Humans may be drifting away from our natural instincts due to the differing incentives of the modern capitalist world: Lower marriage rate, lower fertility, etc. Worsening teenage mental health may also be a sign of “underlying unhealthiness”/goodharting in some way. And increasing political polarization could be interpreted as human social instincts getting hijacked by larger entities (“egregores”) into larger-scale, more optimally power-seeking conflict. (Of course, there are other possible hypotheses for all these, and they’re completely independent from the main gist of the post).</span></p><p><span>And AI replacing us is obviously another option.</span></p><p><span>Also, I’d be remiss not to mention Ben Garfinkel’s blogpost on another concrete mechanism for how our current Golden Age could come to an end: </span><a href="https://forum.effectivealtruism.org/posts/TMCWXTayji7gvRK9p/is-democracy-a-fad"><span>Is Democracy A Fad?</span></a></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/FxHzT6jeTRhbkzSX3/the-darwinian-honeymoon-why-i-am-not-as-impressed-by-human-1#comments">Discuss</a>

### [Sawtooth Problems](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iyLirpAeQotmZK4QC/sawtooth-problems)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><span>Red Button, Blue Button</span></h1><p><span>On April 24th, 2026, Tim Urban put forth the following poll </span><a href="https://x.com/waitbutwhy/status/2047710215265730755" rel="noreferrer"><span>on Twitter/X</span></a><span>:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Everyone in the world has to take a private vote by pressing a red or blue button. If more than 50% of people press the blue button, everyone survives. If less than 50% of people press the blue button, only people who pressed the red button survive. Which button would you press?</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I love this dilemma, and I'm exhausted by it. I’ve been thinking about it for two straight weeks, and have spent nearly all that time refining my thoughts by writing this piece. It's </span><i><span>consumed</span></i><span> me in a way that I've never before experienced with any math problem, and I need to get it out of my head.</span></p><p><span>Discourse surrounding the Button Dilemma reminds me of polarizingly political topics. In much the same way that political discussions make people </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/9weLK2AJ9JEt2Tt8f" rel="noreferrer"><span>go funny in the head</span></a><span>, answers to the Button Dilemma tend to elicit vitriol from people of both Red and Blue conviction. Everyone feels their answer is clear, and everyone is confounded by the lack of consensus.</span></p><p><span>I think this dilemma is pointing to something very important and fundamental about coordination problems. The variety of answers it receives, the arguments, and the emotional fervor feel </span><i><span>raw</span></i><span> in a way that I've never seen so cleanly extricated from political policy arguments.</span></p><p><span>There's something very strange happening inside the Button Dilemma's universe, and the more I stare at it, the more I find.</span></p><p><span>I'd like to share some of my observations.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefys9apvpi58a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnys9apvpi58a">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>What Are We Even Arguing About?</span></h1><p><span>I see a common thread whereby the Button Dilemma is approached as a sort of lateral thinking puzzle. Is everyone awake and lucid? Are blind people forced to pick at random? How do we account for people who don’t have hands and can’t press buttons? Are </span><i><span>children </span></i><span>expected to choose a button? Well if </span><i><span>children </span></i><span>are involved, then I’m </span><i><span>definitely</span></i><span> choosing blue!</span></p><p><span>I endeavor to omit these sorts of questions from the exercise we're about to walk through.</span></p><p><span>If you find yourself wondering about questions like these, I encourage you to reinterpret the scenario such that your concern has been nullified. All button-pressers are lucid. Blind people are provided with braille signs that describe each button unambiguously. Children do not participate.</span></p><p><span>The answer to the Trolley Problem is not “what if the five people are terrorists," and the answer to the Button Dilemma is not “what if some participants are children.”</span></p><p><span>In exploring most philosophical or mathematical problems, the act of </span><i><span>reframing </span></i><span>can often help guide us toward improved mental models. When we reframe a problem, we're trying to see it from different angles, and one of the easiest ways to reframe a problem is to consider it in a real world context. </span></p><p><span>I think it might be the case that when you reframe the Button Dilemma, you fundamentally change the question being asked.</span></p><p><span>This made me uncomfortable at first. On the Button Dilemma, I feel myself a strong, confident Blue. I want to coordinate, and I'm predisposed toward expecting that others want to coordinate by default. I have a </span><i><span>Blue prior</span></i><span>. I want everyone to live. Some of my friends might press Blue, but that almost doesn't matter: I just want everyone to be okay, and pressing Blue seems like the only realistic way to achieve a guarantee that everyone will be okay.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Then I caught a glimpse of what it looks like to want to press Red.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>If you are Red in the Button Dilemma, I expect this to feel very familiar. If you know in your heart that you are Blue, and will always be a Blue, I invite you to join me in feeling what it's like to be on the Red team.</span></p><p><span>Abandon the button.</span></p><p><span>Everyone in the world is teleported into their own private room containing a human-sized blender. If &gt;50% of people enter their blender, everyone survives. If &lt;=50% of people enter their blender, only people who stayed out of their blender survive.</span></p><p><span>Will you enter your blender?</span></p><p><span>This feels like an isomorphic re-framing of the Button Dilemma. It asks the same question: will you risk your life, that others may live? Or will you preserve your own safety, and hope your loved ones join you in survival? Stepping into the blender is Blue; staying out is Red.</span></p><p><span>Philosophically it seems to be the same kind of question, but it feels </span><i><span>very </span></i><span>different for some of us Blues. There's something </span><i><span>obvious</span></i><span> about this choice of Red. All of a sudden, Red no longer feels like </span><i><span>defection</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>Of </span><i><span>course</span></i><span> you don't get into the blender, right? And your loved ones won't either, right? It would be </span><i><span>stupid</span></i><span> to get in the blender. We're all coordinating on not-the-blender, right everyone? </span><i><span>Right? Why would anyone get in the blender?</span></i></p><p><span>In spite of everything, Red starts to feel like a kind of coordination. In the back of your mind, you nervously expect there to be some death, but hopefully not very much. </span><i><span>Surely</span></i><span> not very much.</span></p><p><span>You don't want death. You really don't. But you just can't bring yourself to accept that we could possibly be coordinating on stepping into the blender. Stepping into the blender is practically committing </span><i><span>suicide</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>Then the final outcome is revealed, and you discover that 58% of people stepped into the blender.</span></p><p><span>You feel a sort of weird astonishment. In one sense you're relieved, because everyone survived! But then the ugly, strange fact hits you with full force: 58% of your peers chose to step into a fucking blender. Worse still, a bunch of them are now asking why you willfully risked their lives by staying out of the blender. They want reassurance that you've learned your lesson, and you'll step into the blender next time. "So long as we all do it, it's safe," they say.</span></p><p><span>But this does not feel safe. It feels like a </span><i><span>threat</span></i><span>. You don't want to go into the blender. Why on Earth would you ever do that? The blender is a risk. The blender is death.</span></p><p><i><span>Oh my God.</span></i></p><p><span>This was the experience that broke me, broke my sense of confidence in my own </span><i><span>morality</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>With the flip of a switch, I had become Red. I could flip it back, too. In the Button Dilemma, I press the Blue button, and I look over at those who press Red with deep suspicion and fear. In the Blender Dilemma, I stay out of the goddamn blender.</span></p><p><span>It feels hypocritical in an egregious kind of way. It's like I'm lying to myself, and I can see that I'm lying but I can't </span><i><span>stop</span></i><span>. It feels like going insane.</span></p><p><span>Earlier I said "some of us Blues" give different answers for the blender and the button, but I'm not sure how many like-minded Blues feel as bothered by this unresolved hypocrisy as I do.</span></p><p><span>So, I've set out to resolve it.</span></p><p><span>My central thesis can be summarized like this: I think I've found an undescribed and extremely useful way to group and characterize a larger category of coordination problem from which the Button Dilemma emerges, and I think it might be important.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6qu5sbbqmwl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6qu5sbbqmwl">[2]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>A Fair Way To Look At It</span></h1><p><span>If we want to talk about the general kind of coordination problem that the Button Dilemma seems to be involved with, we need a way to see the world it lives in. What is it </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> asking us, and are there non-obvious ways to reframe this class of problem while retaining its crux—without turning a button into a blender? Are we actually trying to coordinate, here? Is coordination Blue, and defection Red? Does it always </span><i><span>benefit</span></i><span> us to coordinate?</span></p><p><span>Approaching these questions from a neutral standpoint is </span><i><span>super</span></i><span> difficult, especially when the various possible outcomes tend to get described as e.g. "[Blues/Reds] are taking an unnecessary risk! Every death is the fault of [Reds/Blues]!"</span></p><p><span>Nevertheless we must try, or we won't ever move beyond the object-level formulation.</span></p><p><span>In moments like this, it can feel tempting to reach for your favorite decision theory.</span><br /><span>Ol' reliable. Never lets you down.</span></p><p><span>Please, put all of that aside for a moment. I promise I'm not trying to attack anyone's decision theory. I just want to draw attention to some strange parts of the territory you're trying to make decisions within.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Here's my attempt at formalizing the Button Dilemma:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefxqtty1v2dtm"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnxqtty1v2dtm">[3]</a></sup></span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Given </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> participants and threshold </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, each participant selects </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be the fraction selecting </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></li><li value="3"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, everyone survives. </span></li><li value="4"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, exactly those who selected </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> survive.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span>Let's graph this. We'll examine a model population of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>:</span></p><img alt="A graph of survival rate as a function of the number of people who press the blue button." src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777658860/lexical_client_uploads/jesfa6kucoineaqsrdj2.svg" /><p><span>I call this a </span><b><span>sawtooth graph</span></b><span>, named for the shape of the contour outlined by the bars. Sawtooth graphs are visualizations of </span><b><span>sawtooth problems.</span></b></p><p><span>Each vertical bar illustrates a possible outcome. If 10 participants select the Blue Button, then 50% of all participants survive. If 11 select the Blue Button, then all 20 participants survive.</span></p><p><span>The graph's left side represents every possible Red victory—all outcomes where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>The graph's right side represents every possible Blue victory—all outcomes where  </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Notice that two different kinds of strategy yield a survival rate of 100%:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span class="math-tex"></span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span></li></ul><p><span>The first case was already colored blue, and comprises the large plateau on the right-hand side of the sawtooth. Here's what it looks like with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> colored blue as well.</span></p><img alt="blue_frame_n20_match_far_column.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777735424/lexical_client_uploads/ynf2bwthjpkesgszxsnw.svg" /><p><span>"Wait, this isn't a fair representation," I hear Reds object. "From our perspective, we </span><i><span>also</span></i><span> want a survival rate of 100%!"</span></p><p><span>Okay, I agree. This does feel a bit unjust, for that exact reason. Here's a third color scheme, depicting Red's preference for 100% survival:</span></p><img alt="all_red_frame_n20.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777743316/lexical_client_uploads/ewowjcrrdvqziuggufzl.svg" /><p><span>Well.</span></p><p><span>This doesn't look quite right either, does it?</span></p><p><span>Now </span><i><span>every</span></i><span> outcome is colored red, as though the Reds are responsible for </span><i><span>literally every</span></i><span> outcome. This is just obviously wrong.</span></p><p><span>We could keep cycling through these. There's a color scheme that depicts 100% survival in red and &lt;100% in blue, and there's one that assigns responsibility for every single outcome to the Blue team. We could also swap the colors, with blue on the slope and red on the plateau.</span></p><p><span>Let's not do any of this. All of these graphs feel like unfair representations, and I posit that the unfairness is born from insufficient nuance.</span></p><p><span>Capturing it will unavoidably introduce some visual noise, but I can't find a better alternative. Let's see it:</span></p><img alt="initial_capture_of_nuance.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777905693/lexical_client_uploads/grnp6pvoft6wazgxvsfn.svg" /><p><span>We can now visually distinguish between survival rate and survival </span><i><span>breakdown</span></i><span>, which brings us quite a bit closer, but there's something still missing. In this graph, we see Red's component of every column, but we only show Blue's component for the outcomes Blues claim as coordination.</span></p><p><span>Here's how Blue contributes to the left side of the threshold:</span></p><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777993435/lexical_client_uploads/kqxwwundc3kz0ghmvpc1.svg" /><p><span>This graph clearly identifies:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The Blues who succeed in bringing both themselves and the Reds over the threshold</span></li><li value="2"><span>The Reds who survive a Blue coordination failure, and the Blues who failed</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref69ntf4oh5il"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn69ntf4oh5il">[4]</a></sup></span></li></ul><p><span>Here's another useful way to represent the data:</span></p><img alt="purple_blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777992980/lexical_client_uploads/upcrfmcum6mpawtdi5n2.svg" /><p><span>This color scheme isolates the survival rate. Red and Blue are mixed into Purple, identifying only the boundaries between life and death. This is a less-noisy, lower-information way to fairly display the possible outcomes of a sawtooth-shaped coordination problem.</span></p><p><span>I'll generally try to use the noisy red-and-blue color scheme when I think the nuance is important for what I'm trying to communicate, and the purple color scheme when I think it's probably not.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>Playing With τ and N</span></h1><p><span>Now that we've settled on a way to visualize sawtooth problems, let's get back to playing with the formalization. Recall:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Given </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> participants and threshold </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, each participant selects </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the fraction selecting </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, everyone survives. </span></li><li value="4"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, exactly those who selected </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> survive.</span></li></ul><p><span>Notice that this permits arbitrary values for the threshold τ.</span></p><p><span>How would you vote if the threshold was </span><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2048303613802631240" rel="noreferrer"><span>80%?</span></a><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefk862zoqjaup"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnk862zoqjaup">[5]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="blue_frame_t80__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777914419/lexical_client_uploads/nnqjthqud40kwwogpul0.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>These are just Twitter polls, but between Urban's 50% and Aella's 80%, the contrast in outcomes highlights an interesting mechanism underlying this class of coordination problem.</span></p><p><span>Below the threshold, Red survivors behave as a kind of safety fallback for Blue coordination failure, and there is an inverse relationship between how close the final tally is, and how much room for error the fallback gives us. If Blues </span><i><span>just barely</span></i><span> fail to pass the threshold of the 80% problem, only 20% of the participants survive.</span></p><p><span>I think this might be a different way of describing a false crux hiding inside arguments about the Button Dilemma. One's selection of button acts as a nudge toward either end of the graph, and it looks like Reds nudge us toward more safety in the event of Blue failure, and Blues nudge us toward a safer margin for </span><i><span>avoiding </span></i><span>Blue failure.</span></p><p><span>Both of these feel like good arguments in favor of either Red or Blue.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefayvc0ebupx7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnayvc0ebupx7">[6]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Let's look at one of the simplest sawtooth problems, with N=2.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>You and your best friend have to take a private vote by pressing a red or blue button. If &gt;50% of you press the blue button, you both survive. If &lt;=50% of you press the blue button, only people who pressed the red button survive. Which button would you press?</span></p><p><span>How well do you know your best friend? One of your lives is at stake. Can you predict which button your friend will press? Can they predict which button </span><i><span>you</span></i><span> will press?</span></p><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777933129/lexical_client_uploads/x5ythiloin1jbgjov8x0.svg" /><p><span>This seems very much like a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coordination_game" rel="noreferrer"><span>pure coordination game</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefq9cs5ftlsy8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnq9cs5ftlsy8">[7]</a></sup></span><span> It has two possible outcomes: either both players live, or only one player lives. If each player chooses at random, there is a 50% chance one of them will die.</span></p><p><span>Notice that there is no defection here, only coordination. Neither party wants the other party to die! This feels like some worse version of Sophie's Choice where her children have to flip a coin to decide whether they both live or one of them dies.</span></p><p><span>But in this sawtooth problem, you can guarantee your own survival. You can just put heads face-up, and choose to live. They can, too. For those of you who choose Red in this framing, you might feel that it's </span><i><span>obvious</span></i><span> you just put your coin face up, and it should be obvious to them, too!</span></p><p><span>But it might not be obvious to them, and perhaps you can </span><i><span>save </span></i><span>them. They might even be thinking the same thing.</span></p><p><span>For tacit coordination in the real world, we don't typically choose at random.</span><br /><span>We strategize. We try to model each others' minds, and our decisions try to account for our best guess at what we think the other party might choose.</span></p><p><span>Do you and your best friend know each other well enough to ensure you both survive? </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmxoogb22b3d"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmxoogb22b3d">[8]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>Extreme Sawtooth Problems</span></h1><p><br /></p><p><span>These next two sawtooth problems have respective τ values of 99% and of 1%.</span><br /><span>I've set N=100 to smooth out the shapes and emphasize the dynamic:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="blue_frame_t99__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777914793/lexical_client_uploads/miaro71jvqnves7fpwjp.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Blue has zero room for error, and a near-miss will kill nearly everyone. Which button do you press?</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref119wnif4uffl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn119wnif4uffl">[9]</a></sup></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="blue_frame_t1__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777914721/lexical_client_uploads/ry8kovqtgthgxhwdcqvr.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Look at the little speck in the upper left. Do you see it? This is the inverse scenario, with a threshold of 1%.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>These graphs show us the smallest and largest possible safety margins for Blue coordination failure among any value of τ.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftxbp3nkq7r8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntxbp3nkq7r8">[10]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>When I think about the world hidden behind the coordination failure in that last graph, I imagine the surviving 99 Reds reacting with collective horror and astonishment. "How could we have failed our fellow in this way? We each chose to live, that </span><i><span>all</span></i><span> of us may live. What </span><i><span>happened</span></i><span>?"</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Let's push these extremes one step further.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="purple_t100.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777917227/lexical_client_uploads/d0ertsd66kylceo7fejx.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>When τ=100%, selecting Blue only makes things worse.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="purple_t0.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777917296/lexical_client_uploads/uh0o6sr5lzsz154ouzzv.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>When τ=0, your selection is irrelevant.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>The graphs are visually trivial, but they are telling us something!</span><br /><span>Look at the coordination problems they imply. </span><i><span>Look!</span></i><span> </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreflg8jbn4k5t"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnlg8jbn4k5t">[11]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Look at the obvious and avoidable </span><i><span>danger</span></i><span> we're forced to contend with at τ=100%. For crying out loud, look at its survival ratio when there are 100 Blues: if everyone votes Blue, everyone dies. Come </span><i><span>on</span></i><span>!</span></p><p><span>Compare this to the </span><i><span>utopian miracle </span></i><span>of τ=0%. It's safe under every condition! It describes a sector of reality within which everyone </span><i><span>lives</span></i><span>, no matter our individual choices.</span></p><p><span>With these values for τ, the sawtooth has split itself in twain and given us two unique kinds of problem wherein:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Your choice will impact only yourself</span></li><li value="2"><span>Your choice will impact </span><i><span>nobody</span></i></li></ul><p><span>We've somewhat seen both of these graphs already. They were here all along, hidden inside one of our N=20 representations of the Button Dilemma from earlier:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="purple_blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777919928/lexical_client_uploads/nunxlaj5csbvnw0umapg.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Both extremes are contained within the baseline framing of the dilemma, so long as you truncate the graph at the threshold.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefmkffb2wf9v"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnmkffb2wf9v">[12]</a></sup></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h1><span>Axiomatic Expansion of Sawtooth Space</span></h1><p><span>What happens if I do </span><i><span>this</span></i><span>:</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefidoux72or5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnidoux72or5">[13]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s+0.50_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777920804/lexical_client_uploads/df0pesadntk9gbqviutn.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>This graph shows something new, capping the Red survival rate at 50%.</span></p><p><span>Up until now, we've been looking at sawtooth problems with different values of τ.</span></p><p><span>But the graph is under our control. We need not be bound by the strictures of a single parameter, which means we can depict whatever we like. I've introduced something new, and will elaborate </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#The_Map" rel="noreferrer"><span>in a moment</span></a><span>. First, let's look at the graph.</span></p><p><span>In this sawtooth problem, pressing the Red button grants you a 50% chance of survival. Selecting Red now represents a </span><i><span>risk</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>Does it make sense to take this risk? Maybe so, but now you have to start thinking about expected value. If you choose Red in the Button Dilemma—if you have a </span><i><span>Red prior</span></i><span>—and if you generally expect the odds of Blue success to be less than 50%, I expect you to remain Red in this sawtooth problem.</span></p><p><span>If, however, you think the Blues might successfully coordinate, even by a small margin, then the odds have tipped beyond every sane interpretation of this specific sawtooth, and I argue that you should then join the Blue team. Red subjects your life to a coin toss. If the odds of Blue success are &gt;50%, then even pure selfishness would still demand that you choose Blue.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>There exists an inverted variant:</span></p><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s-0.50_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777921715/lexical_client_uploads/fpa9e6pnthzwhd7dj6i4.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Things are beginning to look a bit strange.</span></p><p><span>In this sawtooth problem, the maximum possible survival rate has been pushed downward everywhere above the Threshold, bottoming out at 50%. Here, there is no good reason to vote Blue.</span></p><p><span>We don't see the same kind of expected-value calculation that we saw with the previous graph. It is obvious from every angle: in this sawtooth, you </span><i><span>must </span></i><span>coordinate around Red.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>These two graphs represent the same magnitude of a new, </span><i><span>signed </span></i><span>parameter that behaves similarly, but only on either end of the graph. We'll call it </span><b><span>solidarity </span></b><span>(</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>).</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Because we're having fun, let's go wild and give ourselves just one more parameter:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I+0.25.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777922619/lexical_client_uploads/qvmqxncoqe5ln4ziabfy.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>It's like tilting the Red section from two graphs ago 90 degrees counterclockwise.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I-0.25.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777923417/lexical_client_uploads/px5lcgfy7ryzzgdhi0q4.svg" /><p><span>These two graphs introduce a new sort of cliff which behaves much like the threshold τ, but instead of acting as a barrier between the two color choices, it punishes strategies which lean too hard toward either Blue or Red. The sawtooth problem contains an intolerance for too many similar choices at one end of the spectrum vs. the other.</span></p><p><span>So, let's call this parameter, which is also signed, </span><b><span>intolerance </span></b><span>(I).</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>The Map</span></h1><p><span>We now have three parameters to play with, and we're beginning to model sawtooth problems which look increasingly dissimilar to the original Button Dilemma.</span></p><p><span>This might be a good time to pause for a moment and think about what it is we're actually doing here.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Our Parameters</span></h2><p><span>We've defined three forces that can shape our sawtooths.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref54016p595se"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn54016p595se">[14]</a></sup></span><span> Let's review them:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Threshold (τ)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="2"><ul><li value="1"><span>The location of abrupt regime change, bound to the success or failure of Blue's attempt at coordination.</span></li></ul></li><li value="2"><span>Solidarity (σ)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="3"><ul><li value="1"><span>Solidarity imposes an upper limit to the number of survivors on one side of the threshold by scaling survival down to </span><span class="math-tex"></span></li></ul></li><li value="3"><span>Intolerance (I)</span></li><li class="nested-list-item" value="4"><ul><li value="1"><span>Intolerance acts as a sort of secondary threshold. On a graph, this has the result of eliminating entire survival outcomes completely, with the number of impacted bars equal to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><br /></li></ul></li></ul><p><span>Positive values for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> impact outcomes below and at the threshold.</span><br /><span>Negative values impact outcomes above the threshold.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref982sd0q7vwg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn982sd0q7vwg">[15]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>As a reminder, τ is the only parameter I extracted directly from the Button Dilemma. I'm just axiomatically declaring these other parameters to exist because they let us explore interesting hypothetical dynamics of coordination. It's not clear to me that every such coordination problem can manifest in reality.</span></p><p><span>For example, "a Blue majority is the best outcome </span><i><span>only below</span></i><span> some threshold of intolerance" is a very strange shape of coordination problem that I struggle to match against anything in the real world.</span></p><p><span>But.</span></p><p><i><span>But.</span></i></p><p><span>"A Red majority guarantees survival </span><i><span>only above</span></i><span> some threshold of intolerance" feels like it may reflect real-world coordination problems, and it can be </span><i><span>intuitively derived from its sawtooth graph.</span></i></p><p><span>We'll use the graph for that problem below, as we orient ourselves within this space.</span></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Regions of the Map</span></h2><p><span>Many of our graphs share certain features in common. Let's identify them.</span></p><p><span>While the three parameters still feel fresh, and with my hope that this exercise may serve as an intuition pump for something useful, I'll be showing the parameter values for each sawtooth graph from now on.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefivb3skujsrp"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnivb3skujsrp">[16]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="graph_filled_margin.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778067576/lexical_client_uploads/oldplbr1mzbjwgqznfjo.svg" /><p><br /></p><img alt="3_4.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778070314/lexical_client_uploads/xsegapdu3vwyxflvr5c1.svg" /><p><br /></p><img alt="2_4.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778070769/lexical_client_uploads/azy471xyplhwavb5pn7c.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>This is the map of sawtooth problems with these three specific parameters.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref6fnr7mtzv2s"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn6fnr7mtzv2s">[17]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Two regions permit survival:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The </span><b><span>Slope </span></b><span>(i.e. Red survival, Blue coordination failure)</span></li><li value="2"><span>The </span><b><span>Plateau </span></b><span>(i.e. Blue coordination success, Red irrelevance)</span></li></ul><p><span>Three regions represent three distinct causes of death:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Death by </span><b><span>Coordination Failure</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8a2b5lhl2wl"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8a2b5lhl2wl">[18]</a></sup></span><span> (above the Slope)</span></li><li value="2"><span>Death by </span><b><span>Solidarity </span></b><span>(depicted here with solidarity set at -0.25)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Death by </span><b><span>Intolerance </span></b><span>(depicted here with intolerance set to +0.25)</span></li></ul><p><span>Cleaving our map in two is the </span><b><span>Threshold</span></b><span>. It is the defining region of every sawtooth problem we've looked at.</span></p><p><span>I argue that it is not a zero-width boundary. It is a region.</span></p><p><span>And we need to talk about it, because it's by far the most important aspect of nearly every sawtooth problem I've examined.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>The Threshold</span></h1><p><span>Let's go back to the very beginning, to one of the first sawtooth graphs we looked at for the Button Dilemma, with N=20.</span></p><p><br /></p><img alt="Threshold_blue_frame_t50__s0.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778072048/lexical_client_uploads/hun9msnray3bvcelrf5k.svg" /><p><span>You may have noticed that although N=20, there are 21 total bars beginning with B=0. Consequently, B=10 sits smack in the center, and constitutes the Threshold for this problem.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj1phh5yfn8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj1phh5yfn8">[19]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>This graph contains four of the six regions we've mapped:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The Slope</span></li><li value="2"><span>The Plateau</span></li><li value="3"><span>Death by Coordination Failure</span></li><li value="4"><span>The Threshold</span></li></ul><p><span>Whether you are Blue or Red, you might regard B=10 as something that belongs to the Slope. I can't blame anyone who sees it this way. B=10 is just so obviously the lowest point of the Slope, right? </span></p><p><span>When you're only thinking about </span><i><span>outcomes</span></i><span>, it makes intuitive sense to place B=10 on the Slope. But this is game theory, which means we must consider </span><i><span>strategies</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>B=10 is the Threshold of this sawtooth problem.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefj1phh5yfn8"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnj1phh5yfn8">[19]</a></sup></span></p><img alt="identifying_the_threshold.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778169226/lexical_client_uploads/adoqqqepckjfwgdx4hmz.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>The Threshold is the worst possible outcome, and nobody wants it. But Reds and Blues perceive the landscape from different standpoints and their strategies for avoiding the Threshold look quite different.</span></p><p><span>From the Red perspective:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The Blue button represents an incremental backslide.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maximum utility is achievable, but unstable. There is no margin for error.</span></li><li value="3"><span>A surviving population cannot host even a single Blue.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Pressing Blue risks an unnecessary and visible death.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Moving from the Slope to the Threshold is a tragic process, but slow and linear.</span></li></ul><p><span>From the Blue perspective:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The Red button represents a threat to collective survival.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Maximum utility is, by default, a </span><i><span>stable</span></i><span> target. There is potential margin for error.</span></li><li value="3"><span>A surviving population can host a cohort of Reds.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Pressing Red risks an unnecessary and invisible weakening of the margin for error.</span></li><li value="5"><span>Moving from Plateau to Threshold is a discontinuous plunge into catastrophe.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span>Notice that both sides are trying to keep away from the Threshold. Nobody wants to go near the thing, and we all feel as though we're being pulled into it from the other side. It can feel like the Threshold is coercing us into crab mentality, but that's not quite what's happening.</span></p><p><span>In the Button Dilemma, Red and Blue are mutual threats to each other. Pressing Red imposes a risk upon Blue that they feel is unnecessary. Pressing Blue doesn't impose a similar risk upon Reds (the Red take is that Blue is a risk unto itself), but coordination problems in the real world tend to be iterative, and a Blue victory represents a kind of pressure for Reds to press Blue in the future.</span></p><p><span>"You'll step into the blender next time, won't you? Stepping into the blender is the reason we all made it. We'll all survive next time too, and it will be easier with your help. The blender is safe. The blender is </span><i><span>life</span></i><span>."</span></p><p><span>This is not a convincing argument to someone with Red priors. It doesn't read like safety. It reads like a deep social pressure to do something that, not entirely unreasonably, feels like a risk to one's life. Reds can see that the Blues survived, and they might even expect it to happen again. But instead of acting to comfort them, this just makes them wonder: when will Blue coordination fail, and what will it cost me when it does?</span></p><p><span>In other words, this reads as a threat to Reds. "Risk your life for us all."</span></p><p><span>In spite of all of my Red POV explanations, </span><b><span>I'm still a firm Blue on the Button Dilemma.</span></b><span> I haven't even managed to convince myself to switch sides. I just feel like I understand what Reds are feeling.</span></p><p><span>We each see the other side as threats, and we all kind of have a point.</span></p><p><span>Reds are risking the lives of Blues, and Blues are pressuring Reds to risk their lives in turn.</span></p><p><span>These behaviors only draw us into the gravitational pull of the Threshold, which, I remind you, is the worst possible outcome that we are all trying to avoid.</span></p><p><span>This dynamic frightens me. It's like we need to succeed on some kind of meta-coordination problem, maybe even a meta-sawtooth problem. The worst part is that none of this even seems to be our fault: it's the Threshold, acting to draw us in.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>I'll say one nice thing about the Threshold: it feels like a kind of anti-Nash equilibrium. In a multi-turn sawtooth problem, if you find yourself with a group of survivors inside the Threshold, every possible action you can take is an improvement, and the incentive to act is as strong as it can be.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Earlier, we looked at some </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#Extreme_Sawtooth_Problems" rel="noreferrer"><span>extreme sawtooth problems</span></a><span>, one of which had τ=0:</span></p><img alt="utopia.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778168330/lexical_client_uploads/tkhjbfomjegimy53lv9g.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Notice anything interesting, as it pertains to the Threshold?</span></p><p><br /></p><p><i><span>There is no Threshold. </span></i><span>τ=0, duh. </span></p><p><span>The Threshold does not exist on this graph. In theory, it would sit one bar to the left of the leftmost bar.</span></p><p><span>When I look at this silly purple rectangle, my eyes tear up a bit. This rectangle is the problem that's not a problem. This is what a good future might look like with an aligned superintelligence.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref70dfhfj6dgg"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn70dfhfj6dgg">[20]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>If there is a meta-sawtooth problem, and we can resolve it safely, this purple rectangle might be where we find ourselves.</span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>Let's Get Weird</span></h1><p><span>By now, I hope you're familiar enough with sawtooth problems that we can spend a moment just looking at a handful of select cases.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefv7xzai5zxom"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnv7xzai5zxom">[21]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>Tragedy of the Commons &amp; Regulation</span></h3><img alt="blue_frame_t50__s-0.37_I+0.09.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777979925/lexical_client_uploads/sj6tx5ycql6owjpfc5sg.svg" /><p><span>This graph reflects a real-world dynamic.</span></p><p><span>By introducing a negative solidarity cost (i.e. pushing the Plateau down), and by allowing the Slope's peak to exceed the height of the Plateau, we increase the incentive for people to </span><i><span>want</span></i><span> to be in certain parts of the Slope, </span><i><span>even though</span></i><span> this graph's Slope terminates with an intolerance filter </span><i><span>beyond which everyone dies</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>Does this sound familiar?</span></p><p><span>The solidarity cost is the imposition of regulation. In the Plateau, everyone is guaranteed to fare a bit better than the ~10 worst possible outcomes within the Slope, but the </span><i><span>best</span></i><span> possible outcomes </span><i><span>only</span></i><span> exist within the Slope!</span></p><p><span>In terms of pure utility maximization, this shape of coordination problem incentivizes </span><i><span>more</span></i><span> Red votes—provided that the Reds believe themselves to be sufficiently few in number as to not exceed the intolerance filter.</span></p><p><span>Tragedy of the commons, accompanied by overly-burdensome regulation. Choosing Blue feels like losing out and Red provides an opportunity to seek higher utility. But if too many people choose Red, they go right over the cliff. Death by Intolerance.</span></p><p><span>In those outcomes, the sawtooth problem was intolerant of population dynamics with too much Red.</span></p><p><span>This is a graph of a real phenomenon.</span></p><p><span>We just traveled through sawtooth space, declared some parameters to exist, and subsequently found a graph that led to a specific dynamic which manifests in reality.</span><br /><span>A pretty important one, at that.</span></p><p><span>This is exactly the sort of thing that makes me wonder whether I'm losing my mind.</span></p><p><span>In for a penny, in for a pound; let's continue.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>The Decision Theory Befuddler</span></h3><img alt="decision-theory-confounder.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778110754/lexical_client_uploads/g3ttm9twhv2qgebmhjux.svg" /><p><span>I'm genuinely at a loss for what one should even be thinking about in this sawtooth problem. What's the strategy here? What does FDT have to say about coordination problems that impose such harsh conditions of solidarity and intolerance?</span></p><p><span>This is a kind of sawtooth problem I'm not convinced can really exist, so maybe these questions are moot.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefjvb13lafl7"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnjvb13lafl7">[22]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>If Anyone Votes Red, Everyone Dies</span></h3><img alt="if_anyone_votes_red.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778022103/lexical_client_uploads/n1bc8hqt4fif7vshte4o.svg" /><h3><span>If Anyone Votes </span><i><span>Blue</span></i><span>, Everyone Dies</span></h3><img alt="blue_frame_t0__s-1.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777940422/lexical_client_uploads/b44bmyld7hfahtmodyxt.svg" /><p><br /></p><p><span>See those thin bars of life at either side of each graph?</span></p><p><span>These graphs have a 1-to-many relationship with the parameters that can summon them.</span><br /><span>In other words, both graphs can arise from multiple parameter configurations, all of which are isomorphic. But it's fun to think about the underlying mechanism that causes any given configuration to yield its associated graph.</span></p><p><span>Notice the subtle asymmetry in the configuration between these two sawtooth problems.</span><br /><span>The former requires τ=99%, while the latter permits (but does not require) τ=0%.</span></p><p><span>Under conditions of maximally positive Solidarity, and given </span><i><span>some</span></i><span> theoretically-attainable, maximally-difficult goal, a 100% Blue victory is necessary.</span></p><p><span>Under conditions of maximally negative solidarity and a threshold of nil, a 100% Red victory is similarly necessary.</span></p><p><span>Negative solidarity imposes a cap on the total number of survivors of Blue coordination, and by dropping the threshold to 0%, we've turned all but the leftmost bar into outcomes which accept an arbitrary number of Blues. This is sawtooth-speak for "if you choose Blue, you will kill us all."</span></p><p><span>Both graphs actually arise from this same underlying mechanism, for the same reason, despite their parameters. The only difference is which button you need to coordinate around in order for everyone to survive.</span></p><p><span>My choice in either problem feels obvious, but I can't say these graphs don't make me nervous.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>What happens if we tick the threshold up just one more notch on the "If Anyone Votes Red" graph?</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>No Matter What Anyone Does, Everyone Dies</span></h3><img alt="blue_frame_t100__s+1.00_I0.00.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777941579/lexical_client_uploads/sv0xdjqc8jy6vjh5tpmt.svg" /><p><span>This is the Worst Timeline. Here, a mixture of Red and Blue blood drowns all potential futures in pale, hatched lilac.</span></p><p><span>This is a rogue interstellar planetoid crashing into Earth six days from now. This is false vacuum decay. This is misaligned superintelligence.</span></p><p><span>No matter what any of us do, </span><i><span>we all die</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>As with the previous two graphs, there are multiple ways to bring about this sawtooth problem.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h3><span>If There Are Fewer Than 16 Blues, Everyone Dies </span><br /><span>(Except For One Weird Outcome Where Only 5 Reds Survive)</span></h3><img alt="16_blues.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778027548/lexical_client_uploads/rif1ksxhl49ow8mfghly.svg" /><p><span>Okay, these are getting silly. Let's move on.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><h2><span>Weirder Still</span></h2><p><span>Is there a valid kind of problem where the survival rate </span><i><span>increases</span></i><span> by default with the number of Blues, and then abruptly falls to nil above the Threshold?</span></p><p><span>Maybe:</span></p><img alt="weird_route.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778023879/lexical_client_uploads/sfnfjpktfdtxyokach01.svg" /><p><span>This is something we haven't seen yet, because I just changed a fundamental rule.</span></p><p><span>This graph does not depict anything in the same part of sawtooth-space we've been exploring. I'm not really sure it even </span><i><span>is</span></i><span> a sawtooth problem anymore, at least in the way I've been describing them.  It's </span><i><span>weird</span></i><span>:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>It has no Blue survivors, even in outcomes where they are the majority.</span></li><li value="2"><span>50% is the largest possible population of Reds, despite nil solidarity and intolerance.</span></li><li value="3"><span>Below B=50, the number of survivors is constrained by a deficiency of dead Blues.</span></li><li value="4"><span>Above B=50, the number of survivors is suppressed by an </span><i><span>excess</span></i><span> of dead Blues.</span></li></ul><p><br /></p><p><span>This is a world where only Reds survive, and their survival is entirely dependent upon the personal sacrifice of willing Blues.</span></p><p><span>I call this the sacrificial rule. Sawtooth problems which are subject to the sacrificial rule show us the shape of </span><i><span>unavoidable sacrifice</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Maybe this is the rule governing coordination problems faced by soldiers in a trench, moments after receiving an enemy grenade. Will one soldier throw himself on the grenade? Or will all perish?</span></p><p><span>I suspect that this kind of problem is governed by parameters we haven't looked at yet. For instance, the soldier example sacrifices 1 Blue to save </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> Reds. We would need a parameter that can smoothly allow for this.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref0udnk99rklk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn0udnk99rklk">[23]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I haven't thought nearly enough about sacrificial sawtooths, but I nevertheless wanted to include this problem as a small nudge: if you find what I've written here interesting, there's certainly much more ground to cover. The three parameters I've talked about are only the tip of the iceberg.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>But for now, let's stop. We've looked at plenty of graphs. I think it's time to reflect.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefkpcqdomcry"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnkpcqdomcry">[24]</a></sup></span></p><p><br /></p><h1><span>Final Thoughts</span></h1><p><span>My early drafts of this piece largely comprised a handful of loosely connected, meandering philosophical arguments about various reframings of the Button Dilemma.</span></p><p><span>I no longer care very much about those reframings. They almost feel like distractions. Boring, noxious emissions of the Threshold.</span></p><p><span>Yeah, yeah, of course I won't get in the blender. Of course I'll press Blue even when it's the only button. Of course I'll press Red if the Threshold is at 99% and the Slope has low solidarity cost. And </span><i><span>of course</span></i><span> the other side is suicidal/dangerous/stupid/selfish.</span></p><p><span>That's all noise. The signal is in the sawtooth.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefsuqgwyik1zj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnsuqgwyik1zj">[25]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I've not found any prior works describing sawtooth problems as a specific, well-defined class of coordination problem. I've looked, and have found some partially-overlapping areas of study, but nothing that seems to clearly map to anything like this.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref3zc8vn49sxk"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn3zc8vn49sxk">[26]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>So, either I've found something genuinely novel, or I've missed some obvious research on essentially this topic, or I've succumbed to AI psychosis. A lottery which manages to be exciting and embarrassing and terrifying.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefs1d7t95hmxj"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fns1d7t95hmxj">[27]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>I still see myself as fundamentally a Blue—at least in the specific situational framing of the Button Dilemma. But I no longer look across the Threshold at my Red brothers and sisters with suspicion and fear. I fear the Threshold itself. It's among the most frightening concepts I've ever understood. It's a geographic feature of coordination space which puts light-years between us and actively convinces us that the people on the other side are a threat. By convincing us, it becomes true: we </span><i><span>are</span></i><span> threats to each other. So long as the Threshold lies between us, we may always </span><i><span>be</span></i><span> threats to each other.</span></p><p><span>It's like a </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dark_forest_hypothesis" rel="noreferrer"><span>dark forest</span></a><span> that's been here with us the whole time, and we've somehow managed to never see it.</span></p><p><span>So what will we do? Annihilate each other until one side manages to eliminate the threat? Coordinate? Just press Blue?—no, Red!—no, </span><i><span>Blue!</span></i><span> Really for real, everyone! All together at once! No, wait, what are you—</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Most of us are sincerely trying to coordinate. It's become so clear to me that we really are trying. Looking at sawtooth problems has made me feel hope, and it's made me begin to question just how prevalent Prisoner's Dilemma-style </span><i><span>defection</span></i><span> really </span><i><span>is</span></i><span>. </span></p><p><span>I'm not so arrogant as to think I can disregard defection as being a thing that happens. It's just that I'm starting to look around at the groups I perceive as defecting, and now I wonder whether the real issue is that we're all trapped on opposite sides of the Threshold in an especially nasty collection of sawtooth problems.</span></p><p><span>If we can finally see the forest, maybe we can blaze a path. The territory we're coordinating upon is borderline inhospitable, full of perilous cliffs, tragedy, and sacrifice. We need to work together if there's any hope of making it through without destroying ourselves.</span></p><p><span>So if this is a real thing, and if I haven't lost my mind, then I invite you: please spend some time exploring sawtooth space with me.</span></p><p><span>Maybe we really can all be okay.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnys9apvpi58a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefys9apvpi58a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>In the form of many graphs.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6qu5sbbqmwl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6qu5sbbqmwl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I am not 100% convinced that this entire thing isn't just the result of some extremely sneaky AI psychosis. I spend a </span><i><span>lot</span></i><span> of time interacting with LLMs. Hours a day.</span></p><p><span>I do it for work. The scope of my primary job essentially involves building custom harnesses for LLMs and teaching others how to improve their harness architectures.</span></p><p><span>I'm also a participant in Anthropic's model jailbreak red team program.</span></p><p><span>The reason I'm saying all this is: I feel like I'm making some rather grandiose statements in my writing here, despite being an outsider to the entire topic. I don't have much background experience in thinking about math/philosophy/game theory in this way, which has me very suspicious about whether this is all just a load of nonsense.</span><br /><br /><span>I've also legitimately been losing sleep over this thing, and I've spent nearly every waking moment for the last couple of weeks just endlessly thinking about it.</span></p><p><span>My job and my hobbies put me in a </span><i><span>lot</span></i><span> of extended contact with LLMs. This plus the specific way I interact with them puts me under frequent, high-dose exposure. If I lower my guard, or have a gap in my cognitive security, then I'm at significant risk of developing some form of AI psychosis. I'm also thankfully very cognizant and watchful for it.</span></p><p><span>So, if the response I receive is "you're off your rocker," I commit to trusting that. I get the impression that this community would give me an honest, accurate assessment.</span></p><p><span>But I don't </span><i><span>think</span></i><span> I'm off my rocker, at least not for AI-related reasons. For one thing: I've made a very intentional point of prompting LLMs in such a way as to elicit as few remarks as possible on the specific game theory ideas I'm discussing here. The overwhelming fraction of my prompting has gone into building the graphics for this post, and building tools to help me build more graphics.</span></p><p><span>Exceptions to this have included:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Checking my understanding of existing research in game theory</span></li><li value="2"><span>Researching potentially-involved coordination problems</span></li><li value="3"><span>Making sure I didn't botch any of the honestly-pretty-straightforward math</span></li><li value="4"><span>Running a big sweep for typos/grammar/etc. on the final draft, resulting in a handful of minor adjustments to some phrases here and there</span></li><li value="5"><span>I anticipate asking Claude for a direct opinion on the essay itself the moment I publish this.</span><br /><span>After all, I cannot claim I am not curious.</span></li></ul><p><span>Claude Opus 4.6 gets credit for naming the "Solidarity" and "Intolerance" parameters I go on to describe. Aside from that, no LLM wrote any of this piece—not even the em dashes.</span></p><p><span>There is another alternative, of course: everything I've written about could be completely unoriginal and very well-studied. But I've made a very sincere attempt to find significant overlap between my ideas and established literature on coordination problems. And I've come up nearly empty.</span></p><p><span>So either I've found something novel and interesting, or I'm ignorant/blind, or I've got some form of psychosis.
