What fun!</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnxqtty1v2dtm"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefxqtty1v2dtm">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I had to make a decision between treating this as a function of the number of people who select either </span><i><span>Blue </span></i><span>or </span><i><span>Red</span></i><span>.</span></p><p><span>As far as the math is concerned, both Red and Blue are valid frames of reference. I finally landed on Blue because the Red reference frame produces a graph which implicitly communicates something like "everyone survives unless red voters exceed the threshold." I perceive this to be a </span><i><span>very unfair</span></i><span> characterization of Red priors, and I suspect most Reds would disagree with it.</span></p><p><span>For anyone interested, here is what the default Red frame looks like:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Given </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> participants and threshold </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, each participant selects </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="2"><span>Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be the fraction selecting </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></li><li value="3"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, everyone survives.</span></li><li value="4"><span>If </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, exactly those who selected </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> survive.</span></li></ul><p><span>Graphed:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="red_frame_n20_default.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777829313/lexical_client_uploads/jwzzhmwjszdmolw8bmlt.svg" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>🇺🇸</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn69ntf4oh5il"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref69ntf4oh5il">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Arguably, we also see the </span><i><span>Reds</span></i><span> who survive a </span><i><span>Red</span></i><span> coordination failure. Remember, when </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, everyone lives because everyone votes Red. If there is a successful form of coordination around Red, then there must also be a failure mode.</span></p><p><span>More on this in a later footnote.</span></p><p><span>In short, my thinking is that sawtooth problems do not actually have </span><i><span>Blue</span></i><span> or </span><i><span>Red</span></i><span> coordination failure.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnk862zoqjaup"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefk862zoqjaup">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The common threshold for this dilemma is 50%. In Tim Urban's poll, 57.9% selected Blue. Outcome: </span><i><span>100% of participants survived.</span></i></p><p><span>Asked to consider a threshold of 80%, the Blue cohort managed to coordinate 66.5%. Outcome: </span><i><span>33.5% of participants survived.</span></i></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnayvc0ebupx7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefayvc0ebupx7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Though I can sense some Reds prickling over how both fully lean into the Blue frame.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnq9cs5ftlsy8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefq9cs5ftlsy8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>And like a binary coordination game.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmxoogb22b3d"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmxoogb22b3d">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>TBH I expect a majority of people reading this to answer "yes" and, on average, to be more correct than not. I also expect at least some of those cases to involve two Reds.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn119wnif4uffl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref119wnif4uffl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Because I think it distracts from examining the general space we're exploring, I'm intentionally witholding my personal answer for most of these sawtooth problems.</span></p><p><span>I do get the sense that broad and well-understood adoption of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/functional-decision-theory" rel="noreferrer"><span>Functional Decision Theory</span></a><span> offers stable Blue coordination even under this extreme condition.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fntxbp3nkq7r8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreftxbp3nkq7r8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Within the set of problems that permit both Blue success and failure, that is.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnlg8jbn4k5t"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreflg8jbn4k5t">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It feels like a stretch to call either of these "coordination </span><i><span>problems</span></i><span>" but I'm not sure how else to describe them.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmkffb2wf9v"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmkffb2wf9v">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I'm just referring to the general shapes, to be clear. </span><br /><br /><span>The original dilemma does not have any outcome with 0% survival.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnidoux72or5"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefidoux72or5">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Putting the macabre aspects of its subject matter aside, I think this graph is aesthetically pleasing. It makes me feel something that I can't describe, and I really enjoy just looking at it. I would hang this on the wall above my sofa.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn54016p595se"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref54016p595se">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>saw</span><i><span>teeth</span></i><span>?</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn982sd0q7vwg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref982sd0q7vwg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I've got a bit of doubt about the convention I'm trying to establish here, but I like to think of positive values as scaling things </span><i><span>in favor</span></i><span> of incentivizing outcomes above the threshold, and vice versa for negative values.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnivb3skujsrp"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefivb3skujsrp">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Here is a link to the tool I used for making these graphs, built by Claude: </span><a href="https://claude.ai/public/artifacts/f4a94806-a2b8-49b5-a2d6-d403ef21c451"><span>https://claude.ai/public/artifacts/f4a94806-a2b8-49b5-a2d6-d403ef21c451</span></a></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn6fnr7mtzv2s"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref6fnr7mtzv2s">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Not every sawtooth graph contains all of these regions, and not all regions will manifest with the same shape, or on a consistent side of the Threshold.</span></p><p><span>With nil solidarity and intolerance, the associated death regions vanish.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8a2b5lhl2wl"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8a2b5lhl2wl">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Fun fact: across many drafts of this post, long after I felt I had overcome my biases about "who is failing to coordinate," I was </span><i><span>still</span></i><span> calling this "Death by Blue Coordination Failure."</span></p><p><span>Please let the important lesson of my thesis be learned again by both myself and yourself. </span><b><span>Sawtooth problems do not have </span></b><i><b><span>Blue</span></b></i><b><span> or </span></b><i><b><span>Red</span></b></i><b><span> coordination failure.</span></b><span> They certainly do have coordination failure, but it is </span><i><span>not</span></i><span> owed to any one side.</span></p><p><span>(Yes, I'm aware that I literally say "Blue coordination failure" in my description of the Slope region. As far as I can tell right now, this is an accurate description, but I could be mistaken. My take is that Reds don't typically see themselves as having failed when Blues land in this death region.)</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnj1phh5yfn8"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefj1phh5yfn8">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For odd values of N, the Threshold sits just to the left of center in symmetric sawtooth problems.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn70dfhfj6dgg"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref70dfhfj6dgg">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It may be obvious at this point, but in putting myself through this exercise, I no longer see e/acc as defection.</span></p><p><span>I used to see it as the worst defection of all, and I certainly still see it as a dangerous threat to myself, themselves, and everything anyone cares about. But in terms of the people, I just see them on the other side of the Threshold.</span></p><p><span>I wonder if they might be looking up at me and my friends on the Plateau, thinking we don't want them up here with us and feeling very confused about that.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnv7xzai5zxom"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefv7xzai5zxom">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><b><span>An important reminder:</span></b><span> each of the graphs we've seen describes a different shape of sawtooth problem, and our parameters describe that shape. They do </span><i><span>not </span></i><span>tell us anything about the underlying population dynamics.</span></p><p><span>Each graph simply displays all possible population ratios (i.e. all possible outcomes), sorted as a function of each ratio's number of constituent Blues.</span></p><p><b><span>Non-zero solidarity</span></b><span> does not tell us one side is more united than the other.</span><br /><span>It tells us </span><i><span>the problem</span></i><span> imposes an unavoidable cost upon one side's survivors.</span></p><p><b><span>Non-zero intolerance</span></b><span> does not tell us one side is less tolerant than the other.</span><br /><span>It tells us </span><i><span>the problem</span></i><span> is intolerant of populations that exceed some certain ratio.</span></p><p><span>If it helps, you may prefer to think of these as the </span><i><span>solidarity cost</span></i><span> and </span><i><span>intolerance filter</span></i><span>.</span><br /><span>What </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> we say about the underlying population dynamics?</span><br /><br /><span>Well, Tim Urban's poll resulted in ~58% of people selecting Blue, which looks like this:</span></p><img alt="URBAN.svg" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777987854/lexical_client_uploads/g2hpnyvq17ocybvamutb.svg" /><p><span>A single bar represents one population dynamic. In this graph for Tim Urban's poll, the 58% Blue outcome is highlighted—this was the final, measured dynamic of the game posed by his poll.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnjvb13lafl7"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefjvb13lafl7">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>If this sawtooth problem </span><i><span>can</span></i><span> exist, and you find yourself inside it, you're essentially trying to regress everyone to the mean. i.e. voting against whichever side you expect to be the majority.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn0udnk99rklk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref0udnk99rklk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I definitely need to think about this more, because I'm staring at this and feel like I'm misunderstanding something.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkpcqdomcry"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkpcqdomcry">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>There are many more graphs I'd love to share, and sawtooth concepts I'm eager to explore, but they'll have to wait for now.</span></p><p><span>Here's what I plan to work on next:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Pinning down the rules that distinguish sawtooth problems from non-sawtooth problems</span></li><li value="2"><span>Identifying the baseline parameters for sawtooth problems (I suspect the Button Dilemma may be a kind of "most simple" sawtooth problem, and there may be two or three parameters underlying it. τ is among them; σ and I are not.)</span></li><li value="3"><span>Carrying out a much deeper review of the parameters I've defined (at minimum, I suspect that Solidarity might be oversimplified or missing something; it has some quirks I'd like to address in a future post)</span></li><li value="4"><span>Determining which kinds of parameters lead to valid, useful models for sawtooth problems that manifest in reality</span></li><li value="5"><span>Testing some ideas I have for implementing this framework as a tool that can be used to </span><i><span>solve</span></i><span> sawtooth problems</span></li></ul><p><span>In terms of the sheer complexity of what we can accomplish when we work together, humans are the best coordinators on Earth. This has yielded undeniable benefits for our species, and it's also produced a never-ending onslaught of increasingly difficult coordination problems.</span></p><p><span>How do we fairly distribute our limited resources? Which tax structures provide the best balance of resource allocation and individual liberty? How do we stop hurting each other in pursuit of survival?</span></p><p><span>In my opinion, these kinds of problems have been essentially solved—in the sense that some people have proposed rather clever ideas for solutions. But we are failing to coordinate toward implementing those solutions, which means they haven't </span><i><span>really </span></i><span>been solved. We keep fighting about them, consequently getting pulled toward the Threshold.</span></p><p><span>As far as this framework is concerned, I've found two promising ways that we humans may be able to navigate sawtooth space:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Update our priors and change the button we choose to press, thus influencing the final outcome of any given sawtooth problem</span></li><li value="2"><span>Modify the shape of the sawtooth problems themselves</span></li></ul><p><span>I'm especially excited about the second one.</span></p><p><span>Both of these navigation strategies would introduce a third axis to our sawtooth graphs: time.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnsuqgwyik1zj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefsuqgwyik1zj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#What_Are_We_Even_Arguing_About_" rel="noreferrer"><span>Toward the beginning</span></a><span> of all this, I said "I think it might be the case that when you reframe the Button Dilemma, you fundamentally change the question being asked."</span></p><p><span>This line was a holdover from an early draft of this piece. I've come to realize that it kind of contradicts some core elements of my thesis, but it also does a really nice job of cuing up the blender analogy. I couldn't settle on an alternative, so the contradiction remains purely as a pedagogical tool.</span></p><p><span>Situational reframings of the Button Dilemma don't fundamentally change the question being asked, if by "question being asked" you mean "sawtooth problem."</span></p><p><span>But they clearly do </span><i><span>something.</span></i></p><p><span>At minimum, I think situational reframings expose our priors about how we expect others to behave, and/or what we expect the marginal utility of our contribution to actually be.</span></p><p><span>If you have a Blue prior, this might mean you expect there to be enough other Blues for your decision to be acceptably low risk. Or it might mean you expect the outcome to fall somewhere near the Threshold, but maybe it's close enough for your choice to make a difference.</span></p><p><span>If you have a Red prior, this might mean you expect the Blues to fail, or that the outcome will be far enough from the Threshold for your choice not to matter.</span></p><p><span>Priors are obviously not parameters you can adjust to change the shape of a sawtooth problem. They're part of how you arrive at your decision about which button to press, which certainly influences the outcome. This might be an important aspect of how we draw up an operational framework for safely navigating sawtooth space.</span></p><p><br /></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3zc8vn49sxk"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3zc8vn49sxk">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>As far as I can tell, the earliest recorded formulation of the Button Dilemma was posed on Reddit by user Deadshot37 on </span><a href="https://www.reddit.com/r/polls/comments/12t619s/in_front_of_you_appears_red_and_blue_button_if/" rel="noreferrer"><span>April 20, 2023</span></a><span>. I reached out to him and was told that he did not come up with the idea. He could not recall its provenance.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>How about the larger class of problem we're looking at? </span></p><p><span>In the simplest case of N=2, we've seen that sawtooth problems might bear some relation to pure coordination—at least in cases where the sawtooth is symmetric.</span></p><p><span>Sawtooth problems may also have something to do with step level public goods distribution, and threshold public goods games.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><span>As of this writing, I haven't gotten through all of these papers yet—these are just the ones I haven't been able to trivially eliminate from my search.</span></p><p><span>The very first one describes how the moral frame of a threshold public goods game may have bearing on whether people press Red or Blue, but this seems outside of the scope of exploring sawtooth problems in their pure form.</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Schuch, Esther, Tum Nhim, and Andries Richter. 2025:</span><br /><a href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0921800925000308" rel="noreferrer"><span>Coordinating on good and bad outcomes in threshold games – Evidence from an artefactual field experiment in Cambodia</span></a></li><li value="2"><span>Zheng, Guozhong, Weiran Cai, Guanxiao Qi, Jiqiang Zhang, and Li Chen. 2025:</span><br /><a href="https://arxiv.org/html/2312.14970v1" rel="noreferrer"><span>Optimal coordination in Minority Game: A solution from reinforcement learning</span></a></li><li value="3"><span>An, Xinmiao, Yali Dong, Xiaomin Wang, and Boyu Zhang. 2023:</span><br /><a href="https://www.mdpi.com/2073-4336/14/6/76" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cooperation and Coordination in Threshold Public Goods Games with Asymmetric Players</span></a></li><li value="4"><span>Frey, Seth, and Robert L. Goldstone. 2013:</span><br /><a href="https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3575333/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Cyclic Game Dynamics Driven by Iterated Reasoning</span></a></li></ul><p><span>I've also been poking through the following foundational papers in public goods problems. Unfortunately, they are paywalled:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Hans-Theo Normann, Holger A. Rau. 2011:</span><br /><span>Step-Level Public Goods: Experimental Evidence</span></li><li value="2"><span>Charles Bram Cadsby, Elizabeth Maynes. 1999:</span><br /><span>Voluntary provision of threshold public goods with continuous contributions: experimental evidence</span></li><li value="3"><span>Joep Sonnemans, Arthur Schram, Theo Offerman. 1998:</span><br /><span>Public good provision and public bad prevention: The effect of framing</span></li><li value="4"><span>Alphons J. C. van de Kragt, John M. Orbell, Robyn M. Dawes. 1983:</span><br /><span>The Minimal Contributing Set as a Solution to Public Goods Problems</span></li></ul></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fns1d7t95hmxj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefs1d7t95hmxj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This being said, here is what I currently expect about sawtooth problems:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>I barely understand them and have either bungled some of the math or drawn conclusions from inadequately-justified assumptions (99% confident, but I think I nevertheless managed to wrangle this topic into a worthwhile and valuable read)</span></li><li value="2"><span>I've found a new framework for thinking about coordination problems. (85% confident, but this is a fairly vacuous claim)</span></li><li value="3"><span>I've found a new and genuinely meaningful way to group/classify coordination problems (80% confident, slightly less vacuous)</span></li><li value="4"><span>I've found a new cluster of coordination problems which contains a lot of well-understood stuff, but also potentially something quite novel (Maybe 60% confident? This is the kind of claim that makes me doubt my sanity a bit)</span></li><li value="5"><span>I've found something which might point toward an instrumentally useful tool for outright </span><i><span>solving</span></i><span> sawtooth problems and defeating the Threshold (15%?? I'm inclined to be extremely skeptical of this idea, which makes 15% feel like psychosis. But if I hadn't felt this small, scary bit of hope, I don't know that I would have written any of this.)</span></li></ul></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/iyLirpAeQotmZK4QC/sawtooth-problems#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [International Law Cannot Prevent Extinction Either](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Z377spboBjyFAAYAz/international-law-cannot-prevent-extinction-either)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>The context for this post is primarily </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/5CfBDiQNg9upfipWk/only-law-can-prevent-extinction" rel="noreferrer"><span>Only Law Can Prevent Extinction</span></a><span>, but after first drafting a half-assed comment, I decided to get off my ass and write a whole-assed post.</span></p><p><span>I agree with Eliezer's main thesis that individual violence against AI researchers is both morally wrong and strategically stupid. Where I disagree is with the claim that international law </span><i><span>can </span></i><span>prevent extinction. It can't, for the following reasons.</span></p><h2><span>I. International law is largely a fiction (especially when interests diverge sharply)</span></h2><p><span>The analogy with nuclear weapons is a poor one. North Korea signed the nuclear non-proliferation treaty and developed nuclear weapons anyway. The treaty deterred only those who weren't very motivated anyway. And the reason why the US and Russia didn't nuke each other has nothing to do with international treaties (see point II).</span></p><p><span>In practice, powerful countries disregard international law whenever they want. A stark example of this is the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Budapest_Memorandum" rel="noreferrer"><span>Budapest Memorandum</span></a><span>: in 1994, Ukraine surrendered all its nuclear warheads in exchange for written sovereignty guarantees from Russia, the US, and the UK. Russia annexed a part of Ukraine in 2014, and the international community </span><i><span>expressed concern</span></i><span>. Russia launched a full-scale invasion in 2022, and the first thing the international community did was block the bank cards of anti-war citizens fleeing Russia. No military intervention ever materialized. Putin is doing just fine.</span></p><p><span>There are no stable enforcement mechanisms to address violations of international law. This is not a world of parties engaging in good-faith negotiations. It is a world in which Putin, Xi, and Trump treat international commitments as an empty pretext to show off in front of the cameras.</span></p><h2><span>II. The AI race is perceived as asymmetrical, unlike nuclear MAD</span></h2><p><span>The proposed AI treaty is compared to nuclear non-proliferation, but the underlying incentive structures in these two cases differ radically.</span></p><p><span>As Eliezer noted, neither Soviet nor American leadership expected to have a good day if an actual nuclear war happened. They understood that a first strike wouldn't prevent devastating retaliation. Carl Sagan and colleagues reinforced these fears with their nuclear winter research, arguing that even a limited nuclear exchange could trigger catastrophic global cooling. The Soviets refrained from launching a first strike </span><b><span>not out of adherence to international treaties</span></b><span>. They did so because they genuinely perceived the situation as lose-lose.</span></p><p><span>However, the AI race appears to have a different payoff structure, at least as most people perceive it. If you develop ASI first, you potentially win decisively, preventing retaliation altogether. This creates immense pressure to defect.</span></p><h2><span>III. There is virtually zero possibility of consensus on AI risk, unlike nuclear weapons</span></h2><p><span>To be fair, the win-lose perception described above is probably wrong. In most cases, a misaligned ASI is catastrophic regardless of who builds it first. So the true payoff structure is likely to be lose-lose, just like nuclear war. But that doesn't matter for treaty prospects, because behavior is driven by perceived payoffs, not actual ones.</span></p><p><span>Eliezer's treaty seems to require that everyone becomes so terrified of ASI that only a madman would violate it. Is that realistic? I'd say that if there emerges a scientific consensus on this topic, it'll be a great first step towards such a world. Eliezer writes that a few hundred computer scientists, Nobel laureates, and others have called AI an extinction risk. Unfortunately, many others have disagreed, and the public debate is nowhere near settled. The situation is more analogous to the early climate change debates than to nuclear weapons. And even as scientific consensus on climate </span><i><span>did</span></i><span> eventually appear, most countries have done little of substance in response, because their incentives to ignore the consensus outweigh the perceived risk.</span></p><p><span>Without a real consensus on the AI risk, the perceived payoff will continue to be enormous compared to the perceived risk. Trying to build a </span><i><span>working</span></i><span> AI ban on this foundation means a lot of wasted time and effort.</span></p><p><span>At least with climate change, evidence eventually accumulated. With AI risk, by the time the evidence arrives, it will be too late to act on it.</span></p><h2><span>IV. The proposed enforcers have a demonstrated track record of not enforcing things</span></h2><p><span>For a treaty to function, its enforcement has to be credible, i.e., the adversaries have to believe that violations will actually trigger consequences, i.e., the proposed airstrikes. If the US is seen as unwilling to follow through on stated commitments, the treaty is not going to be taken seriously</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefi5fudh6nhcb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fni5fudh6nhcb">[1]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Regardless of the administration or country in question</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefgt4qmrst9iv"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fngt4qmrst9iv">[2]</a></sup></span><span>, modern international politics seems to be characterized by a persistent pattern of maximum stated commitment followed by a face-saving partial retreat declared as success. Viewed charitably, this pattern reflects the objective difficulty of costly enforcement against nuclear-armed adversaries, which is exactly what an AI treaty would require overcoming, repeatedly.</span></p><h2><span>V. GPU control is not analogous to nuclear material control</span></h2><p><span>The treaty's proposed mechanism is control of high-end GPU clusters. This is much worse than nuclear material control in terms of ease and reliability.</span></p><p><span>Weapons-grade uranium and plutonium are physically rare and require large and specialized industrial infrastructure (centrifuge cascades emit distinctive mechanical vibrations, enrichment facilities have identifiable thermal and radiological signatures), and can be monitored with a relatively small number of inspection sites. And one can't simply find a less conspicuous way to create nuclear weapons.</span></p><p><span>For AI training, GPUs are the current bottleneck, where the key word is "current". Training frontier models on CPUs is much slower per chip, but CPU clusters are much more accessible, geographically diffuse, and unmonitorable at scale. A country like Russia (with a functioning math education system that produces new research talent every year) could plausibly distribute training across tens of thousands of ordinary servers in many locations. Unlike uranium centrifuges, small server clusters emit no detectable signatures from orbit. </span></p><p><span>More importantly, algorithmic advancements have repeatedly reduced computational requirements.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreffcdjaka1g5n"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnfcdjaka1g5n">[3]</a></sup></span><span> The one guaranteed result of the proposed treaty is a flowering of new creative, unmonitorable alternatives to the current training methods.</span></p><h2><span>VI. A flawed treaty is not better than nothing</span></h2><p><span>The argument that even an imperfect treaty that buys two years is better than no treaty sounds reasonable in the abstract. Without a treaty, the AI race is unconstrained. However, the flaws in any such treaty will systematically favor power-hungry authoritarian countries, which automatically increases the odds of the </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/risks-of-astronomical-suffering-s-risks" rel="noreferrer"><span>worst possible outcome</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>Which states usually comply with international agreements they find costly and which don't? The Soviet bioweapons program, </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biopreparat" rel="noreferrer"><span>Biopreparat</span></a><span>, continued in flagrant violation of the Biological Weapons Convention from 1975 until the early 1990s. The program employed tens of thousands of people and remained undetected by Western intelligence for most of that period.</span></p><p><span>One almost certain consequence of the treaty is that the most risk-aware AI lab currently working at the frontier will stop its capabilities research. Another is that a certain former KGB officer will be jumping for joy in his secret underground bunker, not believing his luck.</span></p><p><span>If a flawed treaty means that Anthropic pauses and authoritarian programs continue, then the treaty is truly worse than nothing. A quick death from a genuinely misaligned ASI, built by anyone, is a terrible outcome. But there are things much worse than death, and one can only hope that an ASI trained under the supervision of </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Security_Service#Criticism" rel="noreferrer"><span>professional torturers</span></a><span> turns out misaligned and kills us all quickly enough.</span></p><h2><span>So is there a better way?</span></h2><p><span>There is one kind of action that seems valuable in the absence of a good solution: extending the scope of the search effort. Right now it seems that our search should be wider, more desperate, and at a larger scale than it currently is. For example, we could:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>ask billionaires and governments to issue a huge number of grants to people willing to work on this problem so that millions of smart people could stop </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/qRZLEBmNtT6LBuFsE/it-s-nice-of-you-to-worry-about-me-but-i-really-do-have-a" rel="noreferrer"><span>going to their pointless jobs</span></a><span> and try to solve this,</span></li><li value="2"><span>encourage everyone already involved in AI alignment research to crowdsource tractable sub-problems to the wider public,</span></li><li value="3"><span>encourage Anthropic to identify promising researchers among Claude users (Claude interacts with millions of people daily and could, with appropriate design, find users who show unusual cognitive skills).</span></li></ul><p><span>In any case, the absence of a good solution is not an argument for a bad one. We need to look harder.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fni5fudh6nhcb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefi5fudh6nhcb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump#%22Two_weeks%22" rel="noreferrer"><span>recent record</span></a><span> is </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trump_Always_Chickens_Out" rel="noreferrer"><span>not inspiring any confidence</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fngt4qmrst9iv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefgt4qmrst9iv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See also </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%27s_final_warning" rel="noreferrer"><span>China's final warning</span></a><span>.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnfcdjaka1g5n"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnreffcdjaka1g5n">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Recent work on </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1.58-bit_large_language_model" rel="noreferrer"><span>BitNet</span></a><span> and </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixture_of_experts" rel="noreferrer"><span>Mixture of Experts</span></a><span> has already demonstrated that very capable LLMs can be trained on hardware below the H100 tier.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/Z377spboBjyFAAYAz/international-law-cannot-prevent-extinction-either#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Neural Networks learn Bloom Filters](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/buxBdp8NtHGgBwabv/neural-networks-learn-bloom-filters)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <h1><b><span>Overview:</span></b></h1><p><span>We train a tiny ReLU network to output sparse top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> distributions over a vocabulary much larger than its residual dimension. The trained network seems to converge to a mechanism closely resembling a Bloom filter: tokens are assigned sparse binary hashes, the hidden layer computes an approximate union indicator, and the output logits are linearly read from this union.</span></p><p><span>Here's what a small network trained on a toy version of the sparse top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> distribution task learns to use:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="input_weights_for_real.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293136/lexical_client_uploads/fpcytinmqiugrwdeste6.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Weight matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>of a 1-layer ReLU network trained via gradient descent on the toy </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-sparse distribution task below, for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Truncated at first </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens for visualisation purposes.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778311870/lexical_client_uploads/kbgydwwascpofjcxevoq.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Plot of the range of values of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, it forms a bimodal distribution.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>That's the input weight matrix of the trained network. Every entry is either </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> or </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The network has effectively encoded a binary hash for each token - and as we'll show, this seems to enable the network to approximately simulate a Bloom filter, and so output the correct set of top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens with high probability.</span></p><p><span>We provide a theoretical construction showing how to set the weights to exactly implement a Bloom filter. The real network seems to learn to do something similar, and seems to behaviourally act like a Bloom filter, but while we provide a fair bit of mechanistic evidence, we don't yet have a complete mechanistic explanation of the trained network.</span></p><p><span>Additionally, this is just a toy network, so it doesn't directly tell us about what larger models might do. But I found the discreteness of the learned algorithm to be very interesting.</span></p><p><span>It seems to learn a probabilistic solution which scales like </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in width, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the MSE loss. In contrast, a standard JL style superposition solution would have error scaling polynomially in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with respect to width.</span></p><h1><b><span>The Task:</span></b></h1><p><span>As a result of reading </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1711.03953" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Softmax Bottleneck</span></a><span>, I have gotten quite interested in what mechanisms toy MLP networks learn to output sparse probability distributions. I have tried to devise a toy problem to address this question.</span></p><p><span>We sample token indices </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> uniformly at random without replacement in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> uniformly from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></p><p><span>We give as input to a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer ReLU neural network  </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> represents an embedding of the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th token, which we are allowed to choose). </span></p><p><span>Let </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> be the probability distribution over </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> which assigns </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability mass to each of the tokens </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability mass to the remaining tokens. </span></p><p><span>The task is to minimise the expected KL divergence between </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>  and the distribution </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the final residual stream, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Where the expectation is taken over random samples of token indices </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and token weights </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>We vary </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> because we are trying to simulate a scenario where the uniform distribution is being produced in parallel with a distribution specific solution giving logit adjustments </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. It turns out that once we've got a solution that can produce the uniform distribution with no error, we can scale up the uniform distribution an outrageous amount, and then we can make the logit adjustments </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> using a width that doesn't depend on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can be relaxed to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if we want, it just makes it less clean to discuss the network and there are weird edge cases that would never actually happen that have to be discussed, so we avoid it.</span></p><h1><b><span>Construction:</span></b></h1><p><span>The construction below is my hypothesis for what the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer ReLU network I trained on the task above is approximately doing. </span></p><p><span>I had Claude prepare an animation to visualise the construction. </span></p><div class="iframe-widget"></div><p><span>The initial residual stream is given as a weighted sum of the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The MLP learns to turn this residual stream into a discrete indicator set for the union of the hashes. The output logits fall into discrete categories, depending on the overlap of their hash with the union of the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens. </span></p><p><span>The discreteness of the logits allows exactly uniform probability to be assigned to the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens, with rare Bloom filter false positives when the hash of a non top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token happens to lie inside the union of the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens.</span></p><h2><b><span>Formal construction:</span></b></h2><p><span>We use a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer neural network with ReLU activation, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and a residual connection.</span></p><p><span>Fix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and d </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>For each </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, sample a uniformly random subset </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. From the Bloom filter perspective, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> represents the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> independent hashes of token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>For each </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, else </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Add a uniform negative bias </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to each hidden layer neuron.</span></p><p><span>Set </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><h2><b><span>Analysis of a single forward pass:</span></b></h2><p><span>The residual stream initially is given by an input vector </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which has support </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (here we use the assumption that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). </span></p><p><span>By construction, the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th component of the output from the MLP layer to the residual stream is given by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. This is given by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> otherwise.</span></p><p><span>Then the final residual stream is given by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> otherwise.</span></p><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is set such that the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th logit is given by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, with equality if and only if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The indices </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> therefore each attain the logit </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. If no other tokens attain this logit, we will have assigned uniform probability to the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens, and lower probability to the remaining tokens.</span></p><p><span>So we want to have with high probability over uniform random choice of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> that "</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if and only if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>."</span></p><p><span>This is precisely the same situation as that of a </span><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=-SuTGoFYjZs" rel="noreferrer"><span>Bloom filter</span></a><span> (Youtube video that explains them better than I could), also see the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloom_filter" rel="noreferrer"><span>wikipedia article</span></a><span>.</span></p><p><span>The standard bloom filter analysis applies and we get scaling of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for the residual stream width, i.e: to get success with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability, and then failure (false positive bloom filter match) otherwise.</span></p><p><span>Interestingly the false positive case just looks like another token being included in the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> set. So it's a quite robust solution. Bloom filters never have false negatives, so if a token is in the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> then it will definitely be among the tokens given the maximum tier of logit.</span></p><h1><b><span>Training:</span></b></h1><p><span>I trained a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer residual ReLU network on the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> distribution task using online random sampling of token sets. </span></p><p><span>The model used vocabulary size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, residual width </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, hidden width </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></p><p><span>For each training example, a uniformly random subset of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> distinct tokens was sampled together with random positive coefficients </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The target distribution assigned probability mass </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> uniformly across the selected tokens and zero elsewhere. </span></p><p><span>The network was trained for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> optimisation steps with batch size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> using AdamW and a cosine learning-rate schedule beginning at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Training was performed in bfloat</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> precision. </span></p><p><span>The final model achieved total variation distance </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> on held-out random samples, assigning nearly all probability mass to the target top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> set while maintaining low leakage onto non-target tokens.</span></p><h1><b><span>Behavioural analysis of the trained network:</span></b></h1><p><span>We draw sets of tokens uniformly without replacement from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and use these as our test top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token sets as input to our trained network.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778305614/lexical_client_uploads/vuvq53z4v5rfcjxgdlor.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Probability mass assigned to the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens across </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> random token sets drawn from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> without replacement. Note the discreteness of the two clusters separated at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability.</span></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778306039/lexical_client_uploads/cdlv7rxikyztvpxckdjx.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>PDF of probability assigned to the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens across </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> samples.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>So we have a distinct rarely occurring cluster where the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability mass falls below </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></p><p><span>A typical top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> output distribution for a token set in the typical region looks as follows:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="topk_valid.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778306514/lexical_client_uploads/cj6hxb4ef16scf4622lr.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> output probabilities on a typical set of top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens. Nearly uniform probability assigned to the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>After </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> samples of random token sets, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> had false positives, as defined by having probability mass below </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> on the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens (boundary of the bottom probability mass cluster above). Below is a random sample from that set of false positives:</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="topk_bad.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778306644/lexical_client_uploads/wd68iv28ffpga3gixuxp.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> output probabilities when there is a single false positive leading to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens sharing probability approximately uniformly. Green tokens are those in the ground truth top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set, and red are those not in the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>You can see how the network fairly robustly handles the false positive. It doesn't significantly disrupt the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set. If the false positive weren't highlighted, we wouldn't be able to tell which token among the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> it was, which is plausibly due to the discrete nature of the logits.</span></p><p><span>Interestingly, we can also provide </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens in the input, and the model will handle it and give uniform probability among the set of given tokens. So </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is more like a soft limit to the number of tokens the hashes are designed to be able to handle without breaking down. </span></p><p><span>Conditioning on there being even a single false positive, we expect the union of hashes to be large, which means we should expect other tokens to have large intersection with the union. We see this in the above figure, with significant probability mass going to tokens </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> as well.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="miss_1338.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308142/lexical_client_uploads/lwd6ki7xsndu4ex9lwk7.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The hash of token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (defined by the bimodal entries of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) is covered by the union of the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set, so it triggers a false positive.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="near_miss_4015.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308221/lexical_client_uploads/mlu6tjyvaffndwbyovxn.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>The hash of token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is nearly covered by the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set, with a single element of the hash not covered. We see in the above that it gets about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>% of the probability mass as a result.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="near_miss_1.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308442/lexical_client_uploads/kvujnsvhp3eumahmczta.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Ditto for token </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="miss_574.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308698/lexical_client_uploads/vtctjy0kq80zcinlxogc.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Token </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>As a baseline, here is Token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> on the same token set:</span></p><img alt="miss_500.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778308818/lexical_client_uploads/avdegjwxnwgwzyszqzy9.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>In a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> token set iteration I performed, none of them had probability above </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> assigned to a token where that token was not fully covered by the union of the hashes of the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens. It's quite interesting how similar in probability the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> near misses above are, suggesting that there is a discrete probability assigned at each intersection size with the union of the hashes (indeed on other samples we get similar probabilities for near misses.)</span></p><h1><b><span>Mechanistic analysis of the trained network:</span></b></h1><p><span>Now we have seen that the model seems to behave like a Bloom filter, we give a partial mechanistic analysis of how the trained weights help to implement the filter.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="input_weights_for_real.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293175/lexical_client_uploads/nvl8rni3ia9oxspl7arb.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Learned </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> matrix (truncated at first </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens), when training via gradient descent on the top-k task. It is seemingly completely random, with a bimodal distribution of values.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="inmat_10_vals.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293326/lexical_client_uploads/rcczffftjcx6f5p9a69b.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of values in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in the trained network.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>The fact that the positive values vary in magnitude (in contrast to our construction, which uses a constant value </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for the positive values) doesn't change the story very much. Remember we are primarily interested in extracting an indicator set of a union of token hashes, and so as long as each hidden layer neuron fires if and only if it is contained in the union of the token hashes, this story is ok.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="out_weights_for_real.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293202/lexical_client_uploads/gtwdfmsq4vnp2dksboxd.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Learned </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> matrix (truncated at first </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens), when training on the top-k task. The mask of negative values is the transpose of the mask of positive values for the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> matrix (the raw values however are not transposes of each other.)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="outmat_10_vals.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293252/lexical_client_uploads/kqnkpzveiqdq3vjdigzj.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of values in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in the trained network.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><p><span>Since both </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> seem to learn bimodal sets of values, we can compare the two masks.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="mask_match.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1777994039/lexical_client_uploads/uoocsihz0yy9ia5iexx0.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Perfect match between the transpose of one mask and the other mask.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>So </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> learns a mask which is the transpose of the mask of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, analogously to our construction (Where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.)  </span></p><p><span>To connect with our mechanistic story further, we linearly transform the residual stream </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of the network, aiming to look like the one in Claude's animation.</span></p><p><span>If the trained network were acting as our construction expects it to, then </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> would provide the change of basis matrix from the privileged hidden layer basis (Claude's basis :)) to the network's internal residual stream basis. So we look at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the residual stream, both before and after the hidden layer, for a sample input.</span></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image"><img alt="pre_resid.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293357/lexical_client_uploads/tkbznnibx9gpxyxhbjjs.png" /><figcaption><p></p><p><br /></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We arrange the vector of dimension </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> grid, but the rows and columns have no significance.</span></p><p><br /></p></figcaption></figure><figure class="image"><img alt="pre_resid_val.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293383/lexical_client_uploads/fukhifedliwdxaqyohqh.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of values of </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is defined as the initial state of the residual stream. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> corresponds to the first residual stream in Claude's animation.</span></p><p><span>For this input, a value is dark blue iff it doesn't belong to the union of the "hashes" (as given by the mask of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) of the input tokens. This has been the case in all other inputs I've tried, though I have not rigorously proven this about the trained network. </span></p><p><span>Note that, just as in Claude's animation, among the dimensions contained in the union of the hashes, there is a wide range of possible values that are taken on. We have values ranging from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> up to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> among those contained in the union of the hashes. This is because hashes overlap at certain positions and so combine values, as well as due to variation in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>There are discrete clusters in the distribution of values, which seems to correspond to the number of intersecting hashes at each position.</span></p><p><span>The job of the hidden layer seems to be to collapse this range in values to produce an indicator of the union of hashes.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="post_resid.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293593/lexical_client_uploads/njrx1t2c98xotilxjyzu.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span> on same input as above.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>This corresponds to the second residual stream in Claude's animation. </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the residual stream directly before applying the unembedding matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="post_resid_val.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778293657/lexical_client_uploads/km8cgfikdhoffj7woybl.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Distribution of values of </span><span class="math-tex"></span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><p><span>The hash values which in the initial residual stream were significantly above the others have been clamped, and the union of the hashes now lies in a fairly narrow range.</span></p><p><span>To get from </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> to the final logits, we apply </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, which is given as before. So the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th logit is approximately a dot product of the clamped union of hashes </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is determined by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>). </span></p><p><span>There can be a wide variance in the raw values of the hash containing matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> as long as the sum of the hashes along each row (each corresponding to a token) weighted by the expected corresponding entries of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is around the same across tokens.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778301919/lexical_client_uploads/n73cowwkkifyj6gosi32.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span>Values of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, where </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of token </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is defined as the hash determined by the weight matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. We use Pytorch notation here, with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> being viewed as a tensor of indices.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The above is the dot product of the large positive terms in the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th row of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> with the terms we expect at </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> at the final residual stream, supposing that the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>th token is among the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. This is not the exact correct thing to be looking at I don't think, because there is also noise terms from the terms outside of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, but it should be the leading term, and it tells us that the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens end up with about the same logit contribution </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. But </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> logits is pretty big and it seems to underestimate the performance of the model, suggesting there is something missing from our mechanistic analysis. </span></p><p><span>Nonetheless, since there is a gap of about </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> logits between a hash that completely matches, and a hash that fails to match in just a single position (see the value plot of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> above), our mechanistic analysis is fine grained enough to be able to argue why say false positives don't occur with high probability, even if we can't get precise enough to argue it's provably a Bloom filter. </span></p><p><span>We aren't fine grained enough to argue why we get so close to a uniform distribution over the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens, unfortunately :(. So our mechanistic explanation is incomplete here.</span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 85.47252643504531%;"><img alt="bugged_pre.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1778309607/lexical_client_uploads/bg3pvennzm7jnnuufal9.png" /><figcaption><p></p></figcaption><figcaption><p><span class="math-tex"></span><span> on a pathological token set.  Again with the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> dimensions arranged in a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> grid with no significance to the rows or columns.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>What's interesting about the above figure is that it's pathological because the union of hashes is large. The union of the hashes being large means that it's more likely for the hashes of non top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens to happen to lie inside it.</span></p><p><span> But the way that you get a large union of hashes is by having top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> hashes that don't intersect. In other words, on any given forward pass, we want top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> hashes to intersect as much as they can (globally we don't want this, though.)</span></p><p><span>Which is the opposite intuition from usual, where we want to minimise interference between features!</span></p><h1><b><span>Conclusion / Reflections:</span></b></h1><p><span>I find multiple things interesting about the solution to the task:</span></p><p><span>In contrast to other approaches that have been discussed for neural networks handling many more features than they have dimensions in the residual stream, the solution does not rely on with 100% probability having a bounded amount of error </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Instead, it relies on having </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> error with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability, and significant error with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability. Bloom filters are fundamentally probabilistic structures, and with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability they result in false positives (this translates to an erroneous token included in the top token set).</span></p><p><span>The scaling is a lot better behaved with this probabilistic approach. We get </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> MSE loss by scaling the width of the network by </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, whereas traditional JL style superposition would scale the width polynomially in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>Because of the form of our solution to the task, the harder task of specifying an arbitrary conditional distribution over the top-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> tokens (say as </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability can be reduced to the uniform case, with the size of the reduction not depending on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. So the uniform distribution task is tracking the residual stream width required to output sparse probability distributions over large vocabularies.  Indeed training a </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer network to accomplish the harder task of specifying an arbitrary conditional distribution empirically results in a hidden layer identical to the uniform task.</span></p><p><span>It's always been a bit of a mystery to me how LLMs like GPT-2 with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> can learn such accurate probability distributions, and this Bloom filter approach significantly reduces the mystery for me (I'm not saying that LLMs use Bloom filters, there is no evidence for this, I'm saying I'm no longer surprised that it is feasible).</span></p><p><span>More broadly, i'm working on probabilistic methods for performing computation in superposition. This was a fun toy case I found for showcasing the advantages of using a probabilistic approach. I view the result on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer networks as an initial piece of evidence that it's not absurd to hypothesise mechanisms which rely on probabilistic approaches as opposed to purely continuous ones.</span></p><h1><b><span>Related work:</span></b></h1><p><span>Prior work - </span><a href="https://openreview.net/forum?id=DgJqQk6y19" rel="noreferrer"><span>The Softmax Bottleneck Does Not Limit the Probabilities of the Most Likely Tokens</span></a><span> - shows that with </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability when </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, given a random Gaussian matrix </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and a uniformly randomly drawn subset </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of size </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, you can find a vector </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> such that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is constant on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, and such that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> if </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. </span></p><p><span>They find </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> just by finding the unique solution to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> lying inside the row space of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (which is given in closed form). The substance of the analysis is that with high probability this solution has higher logits on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> than any of the surrounding tokens in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (though their proof uses some simplifying assumptions, but empirically it works so it's fine). By scaling </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, we can get arbitrarily close to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> giving </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> probability to each of the tokens in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>.</span></p><p><span>I don't think it's mechanistically plausible that trained networks actually learn to find the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> used in that paper's proof (and the paper isn't claiming it is). It involves taking the precise inverse of an input-dependent </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> matrix. However, it did provide some inspiration for the construction given in this post.</span></p><h1><b><span>Further work:</span></b></h1><p><span>Is it possible to fully understand the mechanism behind the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer model presented here?  I'm personally satisfied that the network is implementing a Bloom filter, but can we prove it? Intuitively it should be easier than a lot of Mech Interp because the hidden layer is doing a quite simple operation.</span></p><p><span>What is the scaling for the density of the hashes that the model learns? How does this relate to theoretical Bloom filter density?</span></p><p><span>What mechanisms do real LLMs learn to output distributions? Modern LLMs increasingly have </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> of similar size to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>, so in theory they could use a single dimension per logit. But there's still incentives for them to use the fewest dimensions they can for producing the output distribution. </span></p><p><br /></p><p><br /></p><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/buxBdp8NtHGgBwabv/neural-networks-learn-bloom-filters#comments">Discuss</a>
